real packet-arrival timestamps from the tape (Hawkes arm, stream $H$) and a uniform
redraw of the same $n_{\mathrm{pkt}}^{\#}$ arrivals over the same support (the uniform
Poisson null $P$); the
further null streams that separate the ingredients of the clustering are defined in
Section~\ref{sec:setup-nulls}. The
support is the observed packet span $[\min \text{arr}_H, \max \text{arr}_H]$ rather than
the nominal window $[T_0, T_1]$, so the null is rate-matched to the arrivals it replaces
rather than to wall-clock; the two coincide on busy windows and the former is the stricter
counterfactual on sparse ones. The null is therefore a \emph{conditional
homogeneous-Poisson} process: a homogeneous Poisson process at rate $n_{\mathrm{pkt}}^{\#}
/ (\max \text{arr}_H - \min \text{arr}_H)$ conditioned on the observed count over the
observed support. Per (window, $N$, regime) we record $p_{50}, p_{95}, p_{99}, p_{99.9},
\max$ of end-to-end latency. Aggregation is corpus-median across all windows; the two
headline curves per scenario are $p_{99}(N)$ and $p_{50}(N)$ under Hawkes and Poisson
overlaid.

The paper's empirical calibration is $h = 1.7\,\mu\mathrm{s}$ (one-way async cross-thread
mailbox cost, per Table~4 of \citet{mayeski_fastsend}, halved for one-way) swept over $T \in
\{2, 4, 8, 16, 32, 64, 128\}\,\mu\mathrm{s}$ so the scenarios span from the sub-hop regime through
the slow-servicing regime. Theorem~\ref{thm:reduction} predicts $p_{99}^{\mathrm{H}}(N) -
(N-1)h - T \le \big(p_{99}^{\mathrm{H}}(1) - T\big)/N$ on every window, i.e.\ a fitted
exponent $\gamma \ge 1$ in $\Delta(N) \approx \Delta(1) N^{-\gamma}$;
Corollary~\ref{cor:poisson} predicts $p_{99}^{\mathrm{P}}(N) = p_{50}^{\mathrm{P}}(N) = T
+ (N-1)h$ exactly in every cell whose per-stage utilisation satisfies $\rho/N < 0.01$, and
a strictly positive Poisson wait in every cell where it does not; the prediction is
two-sided, so a Poisson $p_{99}$ sitting on the floor at $\rho/N > 0.01$ would falsify it
just as a Poisson $p_{99}$ above the floor at $\rho/N < 0.01$ would. Any of (i) a window
on which the Hawkes bound is violated, (ii) a Poisson $p_{99}(N)$ on the wrong side of the
$\rho/N = 0.01$ boundary, or (iii) Hawkes $p_{50}(N)$ growing at slope $\gg h$ per stage
falsifies the paper's model of the simulator.

\subsection{Null streams and the ingredient each removes}
\label{sec:setup-nulls}

The claim under test is causal: the clustering of arrivals produces the queueing tail.
A tail in the Lindley recursion can be built by four distinct properties of an arrival
stream, and the real stream has all four at once:
\begin{enumerate}
    \item the \emph{rate}, the number of packets per second;
    \item the \emph{gap distribution}: how often two consecutive packets land closer
together than the service time $T$, regardless of the order in which those tight gaps
occur;
    \item the \emph{ordering}: whether the tight gaps come in runs (a burst of ten packets
$5\,\mu\mathrm{s}$ apart) or lie singly among long gaps;
    \item \emph{slow rate variation}: busy and quiet stretches at the scale of seconds
within a window.
\end{enumerate}
A null stream is a synthetic stream that keeps some of these and destroys the rest.
Running the identical simulator on the real stream and on the null, and comparing the
tails, measures the share of the tail that the destroyed property was carrying. Each
null is a controlled subtraction of one ingredient, and the design is factorial in the
sense that the four properties are removed in different combinations by different nulls.
The reference quantity throughout is the single-stage $p_{99}$ excess, $p_{99} - T$, and
the statistic reported is the fraction of the real stream's excess that survives on the
null, corpus-median over the 3512 windows. All nulls are built per window from that
window's own real stream, with seeds derived from the session and window identifiers so
that every run is reproducible. The 3512 windows are nested in 276 sessions, so the
corpus medians reported here are point estimates and no interval is attached to them.

\paragraph{Packet-level nulls.} The uniform Poisson null $P$ of
Section~\ref{sec:setup-sweep} redraws every packet time uniformly over the window: it
keeps (1) and destroys (2), (3) and (4) together, so a tail that vanishes under it is
attributable to clustering in some form but not to any particular form. The \emph{gap
shuffle} $G$ takes the window's sequence of interarrival gaps, permutes it at random
(Fisher--Yates, the renewal null already used for the Fano diagnostic of
Section~\ref{sec:setup-hawkes}), and re-accumulates arrival times: the same packet count,
the same multiset of gaps and therefore the same tight-gap fraction and the same
$7.5\,\mu\mathrm{s}$ floor, with the ordering destroyed. $G$ keeps (1) and (2) and removes
(3) and (4). A tail that survives $G$ is set by how many tight gaps the stream contains; a
tail that $G$ removes needs the runs. The \emph{1\,s-binned Poisson null} $B$ cuts the window
into one-second bins, keeps each bin's real packet count, and redraws the arrivals
uniformly inside the bin: it keeps (1) and (4) and removes (2) and (3). A tail that
survives $B$ is a busy-second load effect, not a microsecond one. Section~\ref{sec:results-nulls}
reports all three.

\paragraph{Transaction-level nulls.} The packet nulls cannot distinguish two accounts
of the tight-gap surplus: that the exchange splits one matching-engine event across
back-to-back packets, or that the events themselves arrive clustered and the packets
relay them. Section~\ref{sec:results-transactions} therefore groups every message by its
\texttt{transactTime}, the identifier of one matching-engine event (a
\emph{transaction}), and builds five further streams in which each transaction is a
block --- its packets, their message counts and their internal spacing --- and exactly
one ingredient is removed. Service in these runs is charged per packet at its measured
cost, $S_i = T\,(1 + r(\sigma_i - 1))$ with $\sigma_i$ the message count and $r$ the
live decoder's slope-to-floor ratio, so that a large packet adds work rather than
redistributing it; if the size of a transaction matters to the tail, these runs can show
it. Table~\ref{tab:null-design} lists all nine streams.

\begin{table}[tbp]
\centering
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}l >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{5.2cm} >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{4.3cm} >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{4.6cm}@{}}
\toprule
stream & kept & destroyed & question answered \\
\midrule
H  & the real stream & --- & reference \\
\midrule
P  & packet count, support & gap distribution, ordering, slow rate variation & is the tail due to clustering in any form? \\
G  & count; the multiset of gaps, hence the tight-gap fraction and the floor & ordering of gaps into runs; slow rate variation & is the tail set by how many tight gaps there are, or by their arrangement? \\
B  & count per one-second bin & microsecond gap structure and ordering & is the tail busy-second load? \\
\midrule
TG & every transaction intact, in its original order & the idle gaps between transactions, permuted & does the clustering of transactions into runs build the tail? \\
TP & every transaction intact & transaction start times, redrawn uniformly & does transaction timing matter at all? \\
TS & real start times & which transaction shape sits at which start (shapes permuted across starts) & do large transactions arriving at particular moments matter? \\
TM & real start times & the split of a transaction into packets (each transaction becomes one packet) & does the exchange's packetisation matter? \\
TW & real start times, the split into packets & the tight spacing inside a split transaction (packets spread by the median idle gap) & does back-to-back spacing inside a transaction matter? \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\caption{The nine arrival streams. $P$, $G$ and $B$ act on packet times with constant
service and are reported in Table~\ref{tab:nulls}; \textsf{TG}--\textsf{TW} act on transaction blocks
with span-dependent service and are reported in Table~\ref{tab:tx-arms}. Reading the
results together: $P \approx 0$ and $\textsf{TP} \approx 0$ say the tail is produced by the timing of the
transaction process; $\textsf{TS}$, $\textsf{TM}$, $\textsf{TW} \approx 1$ say sizes, packetisation and
intra-transaction spacing are not the mechanism; $G$ and \textsf{TG}, which remove the same
thing at two levels of description, give the same profile across $T$ and split the
clustering into the two channels named in Section~\ref{sec:results-transactions}.}
\label{tab:null-design}
\end{table}

The logic of the two families is the same and they are read together. If $P$ and \textsf{TP}
remove the tail, the tail is produced by the timing of the transaction process. If \textsf{TS}, \textsf{TM} and
\textsf{TW} leave it, the size of transactions, their division into packets, and the spacing of
those packets are not the mechanism, and the exchange is relaying events rather than
creating bursts. $G$ and \textsf{TG} remove the same ingredient --- the arrangement of events
into runs --- at the packet and at the transaction level respectively; agreement between
them identifies the packet-level ordering channel with the clustering of transactions.
What survives $G$ is the surplus of tight gaps over a rate-matched Poisson
stream, which Table~\ref{tab:tx-gaps} measures directly. That surplus shows the gap
distribution is far from exponential; it does not show what produces it, and $G$ itself is
a stream with the surplus and no self-excitation. What $G$ removes, the runs, is the
self-exciting component.

% =====================================================================

\section{Empirical results}
\label{sec:results}

\subsection{Single-thread and tandem tails on the real stream and on the Poisson null}
\label{sec:results-tail}

This section presents the main result. It runs the simulated pipeline over the whole
corpus and reports, for each task length and each stage count, how long a typical message
takes and how long the slowest one in a hundred takes. Each case is run twice: once on the
real packet arrivals, which come in bursts, and once on a control in which the same
number of packets is scattered uniformly at random over the window. The comparison
isolates the effect of bursts. The findings
are these. On randomly scattered arrivals there is almost no slow tail at the task lengths a
trading hot path uses, so splitting a task across threads gains nothing and costs a hop per
stage. On the real, bursty arrivals a slow tail appears once a task is longer than about
seven microseconds, and it grows quickly with task length, until the slowest messages
take tens of times longer than the task itself. There, splitting the task into two or
more stages cuts the tail sharply for a small, fixed cost on the typical message, and the
longer the task, the more stages pay off. In short, it is the bursts, not the average
load, that create the tail, and that is why splitting helps on real data and not on the
random control.

Table~\ref{tab:main-corpus} reports corpus-median end-to-end latency quantiles for every
$(T, N)$ cell of the sweep at $h = 1.7\,\mu\mathrm{s}$, on 276 sessions and 3512
thirty-minute windows (five of the 281 session tapes contain no complete RTH window and
are dropped). Figure~\ref{fig:curves} plots the same numbers. The Poisson-null columns are
the uniform Poisson null $P$ of Section~\ref{sec:setup-sweep} with the same packet
count per window.

table shows: the median column equals $T + (N-1)h$ in every cell but three (Hawkes
$N = 1$ at $64$ and $128\,\mu\mathrm{s}$ and $N = 2$ at $128$), so each stage costs
exactly one hop at the median. Under real arrivals the single-stage $p_{99}$ sits on the
floor at $T \le 4\,\mu\mathrm{s}$, is $1.08\,T$ at $8\,\mu\mathrm{s}$, and then grows
faster than the service time: $2.2\,T$, $4.6\,T$, $12.8\,T$ and $41.5\,T$ at $T = 16$,
$32$, $64$ and $128\,\mu\mathrm{s}$, with $p_{99.9}$ roughly twice the $p_{99}$ excess
again. Under the Poisson null the single-stage $p_{99}$ equals the median up to
$16\,\mu\mathrm{s}$ and is $1.3\,T$, $1.7\,T$ and $1.9\,T$ at $32$, $64$ and $128$. One
cut ($N = 2$) takes the Hawkes $p_{99}$ from $34.6$ to $18.3$ at $16\,\mu\mathrm{s}$,
from $146$ to $52$ at $32$, from $821$ to $180$ at $64$ and from $5317$ to $886$ at
$128$, for a median cost of $1.7\,\mu\mathrm{s}$; further cuts continue to lower the
tail until the per-stage service falls below the floor, after which every quantile rises
by $h$ per stage. Below about $8\,\mu\mathrm{s}$ there is no queue to
shorten and splitting is cost only, under either arrival law; from $16\,\mu\mathrm{s}$
the real stream carries a tail that the Poisson stream at the same rate does not, and
splitting removes most of it for one hop; the Poisson tail that appears from
$32\,\mu\mathrm{s}$ is ordinary utilisation queueing ($\rho = \bar\lambda T$ reaching
$0.01$--$0.05$) and is $11$ to $47$ times smaller than the real one. The reason is the
arrival process: a deterministic server queues only when consecutive gaps are shorter than
its service time, the real stream has four to seventeen times the Poisson share of such
gaps (Table~\ref{tab:tx-gaps}) and arranges them in runs, and a cut halves the service
each stage must fit into a gap, so the number of gaps that queue, and the length of the
runs that compound, both fall with $N$ (Theorem~\ref{thm:reduction}).}
\label{tab:main-corpus}
\end{table}

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{figs/tandem_curves.pdf}
\caption{Corpus-median latency versus stage count $N$ for each service floor $T$ (one
shade per $T$, each curve labelled at its $N = 1$ end). Left: median. Centre: $p_{99}$,
Hawkes solid and Poisson null dashed. Right: the Hawkes tail excess ratio
$\Delta(N)/\Delta(1)$ against the Theorem~\ref{thm:reduction} bound $1/N$ (dotted); open
markers are cells where the tandem drives $\Delta(N)$ to exactly zero, drawn at the panel
floor because zero cannot be placed on a log axis. The median rises by exactly $h$ per hop
wherever the queue is empty at the median, which is every cell except the three
longest-service Hawkes cells. The Hawkes $p_{99}$ falls with $N$ only at $T \ge
16\,\mu\mathrm{s}$; at $T \le 8\,\mu\mathrm{s}$ there is no tail to compress and it rises
with the hop count, tracking the median. The Poisson $p_{99}$ likewise tracks the median
up to $T = 16\,\mu\mathrm{s}$ and separates from it at $T \ge 32\,\mu\mathrm{s}$, where
utilisation queueing begins.}
\label{fig:curves}
\end{figure}

Under the Poisson null the queue is empty when almost every message arrives, so every
reported quantile equals the service floor, $p_{50} = p_{99} = T + (N-1)h$, in every cell
with $T \le 16\,\mu\mathrm{s}$. The first exception is $T = 32\,\mu\mathrm{s}$, $N = 1$,
where Poisson $p_{99}$ is $42.0\,\mu\mathrm{s}$ against a floor of $32$: at the
corpus-median packet rate of $452$ packets per second the utilisation there is $\rho = 452
\times 32\,\mu\mathrm{s} = 0.0144$, above the $0.01$ at which Corollary~\ref{cor:poisson}
says the Poisson wait must first appear at $p_{99}$, and it does; at $N = 2$ the per-stage
utilisation halves to $0.007$ and the wait vanishes again. Beyond that the null arm is a
perfectly ordinary queue: at $T = 64$ and $128\,\mu\mathrm{s}$ the single-stage Poisson
$p_{99}$ reaches $106.7$ and $237.7\,\mu\mathrm{s}$, and splitting reduces it, to $75.7$
and $172.4\,\mu\mathrm{s}$ at $N = 2$. Under Poisson, splitting is therefore not useless in general; it is useless in the
regime an HFT hot path occupies. The $p_{99.9}$ column shows the same pattern one decade
lower: the Poisson wait is visible at $N = 1$ for every $T
\ge 4\,\mu\mathrm{s}$ (single-stage $\rho > 10^{-3}$) and at $N = 2$ for $T \ge
8\,\mu\mathrm{s}$, and absent wherever the per-stage $\rho/N$ falls below $10^{-3}$. Every
one of these switch-on points is the $\rho = 1 - q$ boundary of
Corollary~\ref{cor:poisson}, which the corpus reproduces at $q = 0.99$ and $0.999$
(Section~\ref{sec:evaluation}). The null arm is a queue with a small, predictable wait, and over $T \le
16\,\mu\mathrm{s}$ splitting under Poisson costs $(N-1)h$ at every reported quantile.

Under Hawkes a tail appears only above a threshold. At $T = 2$ and $4\,\mu\mathrm{s}$
there is no tail: single-stage $p_{99}$ and $p_{99.9}$ both equal the service floor, the
clustered feed is indistinguishable from the Poisson null at $p_{99}$ and $p_{99.9}$, and splitting costs $(N-1)h$
and removes nothing. The tail appears between $T = 4$ and $8\,\mu\mathrm{s}$ and then
grows rapidly: single-stage $p_{99}$ is $1.00$, $1.00$, $1.08$, $2.16$, $4.57$, $12.8$ and
$41.5$ times the service floor at $T = 2, 4, 8, 16, 32, 64, 128\,\mu\mathrm{s}$, and
single-stage $p_{99.9}$ reaches $97.6\,T$ at $T = 128$. Splitting into two stages cuts the
$p_{99}$ from $34.59$ to $18.31\,\mu\mathrm{s}$ at $T = 16$, from $146.1$ to $52.3$ at $T
= 32$, from $820.7$ to $179.8$ at $T = 64$, and from $5316.9$ to $886.4$ at $T = 128$, in
each case for a median cost of $1.7\,\mu\mathrm{s}$ per hop; the two-stage $p_{99}$ is
below the single-stage $p_{99}$ on $100\%$ of the 3512 windows at every $T \ge
16\,\mu\mathrm{s}$. The stage count that minimises $p_{99}$ rises with $T$: $N = 1$ at $T
\le 8$, $N = 2$ at $T = 16$, $N = 4$ at $T = 32$, $N = 8$ at $T = 64$ and $128$. The $T = 2$ and $4\,\mu\mathrm{s}$ rows, both below the floor, are included to
demonstrate that boundary: splitting there is dominated under both arrival laws. Section~\ref{sec:results-threshold} resolves the $4$--$8\,\mu\mathrm{s}$
onset to $1\,\mu\mathrm{s}$ and Section~\ref{sec:results-conditioning} explains what sets
its location.

\subsection{Gap-shuffle and 1\,s-binned nulls}
\label{sec:results-nulls}

The Poisson null of Table~\ref{tab:main-corpus} redraws every arrival uniformly over the
whole window. That removes three things at once: the microsecond-scale self-excitation,
the slower rate variation across the window, and the shape of the gap distribution,
including the tight-gap floor of Section~\ref{sec:results-conditioning}. The tail
difference in Table~\ref{tab:main-corpus} is credited to the first of these, but the
mechanism of Section~\ref{sec:results-conditioning} runs on the third: a queue forms when
consecutive gaps are shorter than the service time, and that is a property of the gap
distribution, which a reordering of the same gaps leaves untouched. Two further nulls
separate the three. The \emph{gap shuffle} permutes each window's interarrival gaps at
random (the renewal null of Section~\ref{sec:setup-hawkes}): same count, same gap
distribution, same tight-gap floor, no ordering. The \emph{1\,s-binned Poisson} keeps
each one-second bin's own packet count and redraws the arrivals uniformly inside the bin:
same second-scale rate variation, no microsecond clustering.

each one-second packet count and destroys the microsecond structure. What the table

gap in the stream is shorter than $4\,\mu\mathrm{s}$ and no queue can form. At $N = 1$
and $T \ge 8\,\mu\mathrm{s}$ the real stream carries an excess over $T$ ($0.61$, $18.6$,

and the uniform null remove almost entirely (both at or within $2$--$15\%$ of the excess
in every cell, and identical to each other at $8$ and $16\,\mu\mathrm{s}$), while the gap
shuffle retains $88\%$, $79\%$, $59\%$, $37\%$ and $23\%$ of it. At $N \ge 2$ the four
arms converge on $T + (N-1)h$ as soon as the per-stage service $T/N$ falls below the
floor (at $T = 16$, $N = 2$: $18.3$, $17.7$, $18.2$, $17.7$). The tail is
not second-scale load, since a stream with the real busy seconds and random microsecond
placement has none of it; at $8$--$32\,\mu\mathrm{s}$ the tail is set mainly by how many
gaps shorter than $T$ the stream contains, since a stream with the same gaps in random
order keeps most of it; and from $64\,\mu\mathrm{s}$ the ordering of those gaps into runs
carries the majority. The reason is the Lindley recursion itself: a single gap shorter
than $T$ costs the next packet one wait, which the following long gap drains, so a short
service time is hit by every tight gap whether or not its neighbours are tight, whereas a
long service time needs a sustained run of tight gaps before the backlog exceeds one
service, and runs are what the shuffle removes. The tandem's benefit does not depend on
which channel carries the tail: a cut that takes $T/N$ below the floor removes the excess
under every arm. Session-clustered bootstrap over the 276 sessions (1000 draws), $95\%$
intervals of the gap-shuffle shares: $87.1$--$88.4\%$, $76.7$--$81.3\%$, $58.2$--$60.5\%$,
$35.8$--$37.2\%$ and $21.9$--$23.6\%$ at $T = 8$ to $128\,\mu\mathrm{s}$; of the binned
null at $32$--$128$: $12.5$--$14.2\%$, $6.2$--$6.6\%$, $2.2$--$2.4\%$.}
\label{tab:nulls}
\end{table}

\caption{Table~\ref{tab:nulls} as bars: the share of the single-thread $p_{99}$ excess

shuffle (green), which keeps every gap and destroys their order, keeps most of the tail at
$8$--$16\,\mu\mathrm{s}$ and less as $T$ grows: at short service times the tight gaps carry
the tail, at long ones their arrangement into runs. The uniform and 1\,s-binned streams
keep almost none at any $T$.}
\label{fig:bars-nulls}
\end{figure}

Table~\ref{tab:nulls} gives the corpus-median $p_{99}$ under all four arrival arms. The
share of the single-stage $p_{99}$ excess over $T$ that survives the gap shuffle is $88\%$
at $T = 8\,\mu\mathrm{s}$, $79\%$ at $16$ and $59\%$ at $32\,\mu\mathrm{s}$, falling to
$37\%$ at $64$ and $23\%$ at $128\,\mu\mathrm{s}$. The 1\,s-binned null keeps none of it
at $8$ and $16\,\mu\mathrm{s}$ and $13\%$, $6\%$ and $2\%$ at $32$, $64$ and
$128\,\mu\mathrm{s}$, within a few points of the uniform null. Two readings follow. The
tail is not second-scale load: a null that keeps every one-second packet count has no
tail where the real stream has one. And at the service times of an HFT hot path the tail
is set mainly by the gap \emph{distribution} --- the surplus of gaps shorter than the
service time over what a Poisson stream at the same rate carries --- with the
\emph{ordering} of those gaps into runs supplying the remaining fifth at $16\,\mu\mathrm{s}$
and the majority only from $64\,\mu\mathrm{s}$ upward. Section~\ref{sec:results-transactions}
traces both to the timing of matching-engine transactions: the surplus to
consecutive transactions sent back to back at the publisher's spacing
(Table~\ref{tab:two-clocks}), the ordering to runs of them, which is the self-exciting
component. The attribution to clustering stands in that sense; which
process places two transactions within a few microseconds of each other is not
identified by any stream run here (Section~\ref{sec:concl-cannot}).


\subsection{Grouping packets into matching-engine transactions}
\label{sec:results-transactions}

The nulls of Section~\ref{sec:results-nulls} act on packet times. A packet is the
exchange's unit, not the market's. CME MDP3 publishes each matching-engine event --- a
\emph{transaction}, identified by the common \texttt{transactTime} carried by every message
it generates --- as one or more UDP datagrams, and a datagram may carry the end of one
transaction and the start of the next. Two accounts of the tight-gap surplus are therefore
open. Either the exchange splits transactions across back-to-back packets, in which case
the clustering is an artefact of packetisation and a receiver that reassembled transactions
would see none of it; or the transactions themselves arrive clustered and the packets relay
them. The corpus decides between the two. Every message in the 3512 windows was grouped by
\texttt{transactTime} and every packet labelled with the transactions it carries:
$3.30 \times 10^9$ packets, $3.55 \times 10^9$ messages, $3.24 \times 10^9$ transactions;
$3.0\%$ of packets (median window) carry parts of two transactions --- the gateway
coalesces transactions that arrive within its emission interval into one datagram, which
Section~\ref{sec:crossval-span} takes up.

\paragraph{Consecutive transactions and the gap between them.} A \emph{transaction} is one
matching-engine event: all messages that carry the same \texttt{transactTime}. One incoming
order that trades against three resting orders is one transaction, however many messages
it produces. Within a session the NQ front-month transactions are put in the order the
engine processed them, $A, B, C, D, \ldots$; two transactions are \emph{consecutive} when
one directly follows the other in that order, with no other NQ front-month transaction
between them ($A$ and $B$, $B$ and $C$, and so on). The \emph{gap between consecutive
transactions}, their inter-arrival time, is the time from one to the next. It is measured
on one of two clocks, always named: the matching engine's (the difference of the two
\texttt{transactTime}s) or the publisher's (the difference of the \texttt{sendingTime}s of
the packets that carry them, zero if one packet carries both). A session with $m$
transactions has $m - 1$ such gaps. Every statement in the paper about how close
transactions are is a statement about these gaps; a \emph{tight} gap is one below
$16\,\mu\mathrm{s}$.

\begin{table}[H]
\centering
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}l rrrrrrrr@{}}
\toprule
 & 1 & 2 & 3 & 4 & 5 & 6--10 & 11--20 & 21+ \\
\midrule
messages per packet      & 95.897 & 2.439 & 0.869 & 0.401 & 0.188 & 0.171 & 0.027 & 0.009 \\
packets per transaction  & 98.295 & 1.689 & 0.011 & 0.002 & 0.001 & 0.001 & 0.000 & 0.000 \\
messages per transaction & 95.362 & 2.729 & 0.822 & 0.558 & 0.195 & 0.280 & 0.038 & 0.016 \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\caption{Size distributions, percent of the pooled corpus ($3.30 \times 10^9$ packets,
$3.24 \times 10^9$ transactions). A transaction is one packet $98.3\%$ of the time and
two $1.7\%$ of the time; three or more packets is about $1.5$ transactions in ten thousand. The
message count per transaction is the message count per packet with the split
transactions folded back in.}
\label{tab:tx-counts}
\end{table}

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{figs/tx/tx_counts.pdf}
\caption{The three size distributions of Table~\ref{tab:tx-counts} on a logarithmic
share axis, counts $1$--$60$. Messages per packet and per transaction have the same
geometric decay; packets per transaction has almost none beyond two.}
\label{fig:tx-counts}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{Packetisation and the tight gaps.} Table~\ref{tab:tx-counts}
gives the three size distributions, and Figure~\ref{fig:tx-counts} plots them. A split transaction is rare, and when it occurs its
packets are not back-to-back: the gap between two packets of the same transaction has a
corpus-median $p_{50}$ of $15\,\mu\mathrm{s}$, $1.6\%$ of such gaps are shorter than
$10\,\mu\mathrm{s}$ and $0.1\%$ shorter than the $7.5\,\mu\mathrm{s}$ floor; a
multi-packet transaction spans $15\,\mu\mathrm{s}$ at the median and $110\,\mu\mathrm{s}$
at $p_{99}$ from first to last packet (Figure~\ref{fig:tx-gaps}, left, and
Figure~\ref{fig:tx-starts}, right). The tight gaps sit between transactions
(Table~\ref{tab:tx-gaps}).

\centering

\begin{tabular}{@{}r rrr@{}}
\toprule
gap shorter than & share of all packet gaps & of which inside one transaction & Poisson at the same rate \\
\midrule

$17.8\,\mu\mathrm{s}$  & $20.6\%$ & $5.0\%$ & $1.24\%$ \\

$2.15\%$ of all packet gaps are shorter than the floor, $20.6\%$ shorter than about
$16\,\mu\mathrm{s}$ and $34.1\%$ shorter than about $32\,\mu\mathrm{s}$; a Poisson
stream at the same rate would carry $0.56\%$, $1.24\%$ and $2.46\%$, so the real stream
has four, seventeen and fourteen times the Poisson share of gaps below the three service
times of interest. Of the gaps below the floor, $0.1\%$ lie between two packets of the
same transaction; below $16$ and $32\,\mu\mathrm{s}$ the shares are $5.0\%$ and
$4.6\%$. The tight gaps that make a deterministic server queue are overwhelmingly gaps
between different matching-engine events, not the exchange's division of one event into
several datagrams, so a receiver that reassembled transactions before decoding would
remove at most $5\%$ of the gaps that queue and almost none below the floor; and the surplus of tight
gaps over Poisson, which Table~\ref{tab:nulls} shows carries most of the tail at
$8$--$16\,\mu\mathrm{s}$, is a property of when transactions occur relative to each
other. The reason the surplus exists is the self-excitation established in
Table~\ref{tab:tx-fano}: a transaction raises the probability of another within tens of
microseconds, which puts far more mass at short gaps than a Poisson stream at the same
mean rate carries.}
\label{tab:tx-gaps}
\end{table}

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{figs/tx/tx_gaps.pdf}

$54\%$ under $16\,\mu\mathrm{s}$, $96\%$ under $64\,\mu\mathrm{s}$; $n = 5.6 \times 10^7$
gaps), closer than the gap between consecutive packets of different transactions (black: median
about $80\,\mu\mathrm{s}$, spread from the $7.5\,\mu\mathrm{s}$ floor to seconds;
$n = 3.24 \times 10^9$ gaps, $98.3\%$ of all gaps) --- but almost never as close as the floor ($0.1\%$).
Below $10\,\mu\mathrm{s}$ the blue curve holds $1.6\%$ of its gaps and below
$7.5\,\mu\mathrm{s}$ $0.1\%$; the region from the floor to $12\,\mu\mathrm{s}$ is filled
by the black curve alone. So the exchange paces the pieces of one event at a steady
$15\,\mu\mathrm{s}$, and the tightest gaps on the feed, the ones that make a receiver
queue, are between packets of different events. Right: the gap distribution of the real
stream (black; the left panel's black curve with the blue folded in) against that of a
Poisson stream with the same packet count over the same window (red, dashed) --- the same
null as in Table~\ref{tab:main-corpus}, drawn in gap space. The Poisson density is one
hump near $1\,\mathrm{ms}$ with a few percent of its mass below $100\,\mu\mathrm{s}$; the real
density puts a third of its mass below $32\,\mu\mathrm{s}$, with the edge at the floor and
the mode near $100\,\mu\mathrm{s}$ absent from the null. The panel is the visual form of
Table~\ref{tab:tx-gaps}: the whole region of gaps shorter than an HFT service time is
present in the real stream and absent from an independent-events stream at the same rate,
which is why the uniform null carries no tail below $32\,\mu\mathrm{s}$, and the
$100\,\mu\mathrm{s}$ mode is the characteristic spacing of successive transactions within
a burst, the scale on which the self-excitation of Table~\ref{tab:tx-fano} acts. Axes:
logarithmic gap axis; vertical axis the fraction of gaps per decade of gap length (twenty
bins per factor of ten; the area under a curve over a range is the fraction of that
curve's gaps in the range, total area one, so heights compare shapes, not counts). Dotted
lines at $7.5$, $16$ and $32\,\mu\mathrm{s}$.}
\label{fig:tx-gaps}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{Self-excitation of the transaction stream.} Treating each transaction as one event at
the time of its first packet, the transaction-start process carries the same clustering
as the packet process (Table~\ref{tab:tx-fano}): the Fano factor of transaction counts
rises from $7.6$ at $1\,\mathrm{ms}$ to $105$ at $1\,\mathrm{s}$, against $5.5$ to $17$
once the idle gaps between transactions are shuffled and $1$ for Poisson, and the
exponential-kernel MLE gives a branching ratio of $0.795$ on transaction starts against
$0.798$ on packets, with every window's fit converged. The transaction-start gap density
of Figure~\ref{fig:tx-starts} (left) is indistinguishable from the packet gap density of
Figure~\ref{fig:tx-gaps} and unlike the Poisson curve at every scale. The self-excitation belongs to the transaction stream: the exchange publishes each
matching event as it occurs, and the packet process inherits the clustering of the events.
Why the events cluster is a question about the market, outside the scope of this paper
(Section~\ref{sec:concl-cannot}).

events, not the exchange's delivery of them, that arrive in tight groups; the same
conclusion is reached numerically in Table~\ref{tab:tx-fano}, where transaction starts
and packets have the same Fano factor at every scale and the same branching ratio
($0.795$ against $0.798$), and by the simulator in Table~\ref{tab:tx-arms}, where merging
each transaction into one packet leaves the tail unchanged. Right: duration of a

\paragraph{The tail under the transaction-level nulls.} To separate the parts of the
transaction process that build the queue, each window is re-simulated at $N = 1$ with
service charged per packet at its measured absolute cost, $S_i = T\,(1 + r(\sigma_i - 1))$
with $\sigma_i$ the packet's message count and $r$ the slope-to-floor ratio of the live
decoder ($0.043$ on NQ book, $0.142$ on ZN book, Section~\ref{sec:crossval-floor}), so
that a large packet adds work rather than redistributing it. Five counterfactual streams
are built from the real one (H), each removing one ingredient. TG keeps every transaction
intact --- its packets, their sizes and their spacing --- and permutes the idle gaps
between transactions, removing the clustering of transactions. TP keeps
the transactions intact and redraws their start times uniformly over the window. TS keeps
the real start times and permutes the transactions' shapes among them, decoupling size
from timing. TM merges each transaction into a single packet at its start. TW keeps the
starts and spreads a split transaction's packets by the median idle gap instead of their
real spacing. Table~\ref{tab:tx-arms} reports the corpus-median $p_{99}$ of each stream
and the share of the real stream's excess over $T$ that it retains.

\centering
\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}r r rrrrr@{}}
\toprule
$T$ & H & TG & TP & TS & TM & TW \\
\midrule
$16$  &   34.8 &   31.0 (80) &   20.7 (25) &   37.4 (114) &   34.9 (101) &   35.6 (104) \\
$32$  &  146.8 &  100.5 (60) &   52.9 (18) &  155.9 (108) &  146.3 (100) &  148.1 (101) \\
$64$  &  832   &  345 (37)   &  118 (7)    &  839 (101)   &  795 (95)    &  833 (100) \\
$128$ & 5460   & 1319 (22)   &  251 (2)    & 5218 (95)    & 5035 (92)    & 5459 (100) \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\caption{Which part of the transaction process builds the queue. Each row is one
service time $T$; each column is the single-stage $p_{99}$ latency in \si{\micro\second}
(corpus median over 3512 windows, service charged per packet at the NQ slope ratio,
packet-weighted) when the simulator is driven by the real stream H or by one of five
rebuilt streams, each identical to the real one except for a single ingredient that has
been removed. The number in parentheses is the share, in percent, of the real stream's
tail excess $p_{99} - T$ that the rebuilt stream still produces: $100$ means removing
that ingredient changed nothing, $0$ means it removed the whole tail. The
five columns ask: TG --- keep every transaction exactly as it was, but scatter the
transactions so that they no longer come in bunches: does the tail survive? TP --- keep
every transaction as it was but place each one at a random moment in the window: does the
tail survive? TS --- keep the moments at which transactions occur but swap their contents
around, so that a big transaction now arrives where a small one did: does the tail
survive? TM --- deliver every transaction as one packet: does the tail survive? TW ---
keep the split into packets but spread the pieces of a split transaction apart: does the
tail survive? TS, TM and TW are at $92$--$114\%$ in every row ---
the tail survives all three, so the size of transactions, their division into packets,
and the spacing of those pieces are not what builds the queue. TP is at $25$, $18$, $7$
and $2\%$ --- placing transactions at random times removes three quarters of the tail at
$16\,\mu\mathrm{s}$ and almost all of it at $128\,\mu\mathrm{s}$, so the tail is the
timing of transactions. TG is at $80$, $60$, $37$ and $22\%$ --- scattering the bunches
while keeping each transaction intact removes a fifth of the tail at $16\,\mu\mathrm{s}$
and three quarters at $128\,\mu\mathrm{s}$. The queue is built by
\emph{when} transactions happen, and by nothing about \emph{what} they contain or
\emph{how} the exchange delivers them. The timing acts in two ways whose weight shifts
with the service time. At short service times ($16$--$32\,\mu\mathrm{s}$) most of the
tail is already present in TG, which keeps the number of transactions that land within a
service time of each other but not their grouping into bunches: a short server queues
whenever the gap between consecutive transactions is shorter than its service time, so
what matters is how many such gaps there are, and the real stream has $4$--$17$ times as
many as a Poisson stream at the same rate (Table~\ref{tab:tx-gaps}). At long service
times ($64$--$128\,\mu\mathrm{s}$) most of the tail is gone in TG: a long server only
backs up badly when many transactions arrive in a sustained run, and the runs are what
scattering destroys. This is the same profile that the packet-level gap shuffle gave in
Table~\ref{tab:nulls} ($88$, $79$, $59$, $37$, $23\%$), which identifies the packet-level
ordering channel with the bunching of transactions. At $T \le 8\,\mu\mathrm{s}$ the
real excess is under $1\,\mu\mathrm{s}$ and the shares are not meaningful. The ZN slope
ratio and message weighting move every cell by at most a few points. Session-clustered
bootstrap over the 276 sessions (1000 draws), $95\%$ intervals: TG $77.0$--$81.7$,
$58.7$--$61.0$, $35.7$--$37.2$, $21.4$--$23.2\%$ at $T = 16, 32, 64, 128$; TP $24.0$--$25.9$,
$17.7$--$18.8$, $6.8$--$7.2$, $2.2$--$2.4\%$; TS, TM and TW within $\pm 2$ points of the
reported values in every cell.}
\label{tab:tx-arms}
\end{table}

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.95\textwidth]{figs/bars/tx_arms.pdf}
\caption{Table~\ref{tab:tx-arms} as bars: each transaction-level arm's $p_{99}$ as a percentage of
the real stream's. Redrawing transaction times uniformly (TP, vermillion) removes the tail;
shuffling idle gaps (TG, green) removes more of it the longer the service time. Decoupling
sizes (TS), merging each transaction into one packet (TM) or spreading its packets (TW)
leaves it near $100\%$ from $16\,\mu\mathrm{s}$ up.}
\label{fig:bars-tx-arms}
\end{figure}

Three things follow. The tail is the transaction process: randomising transaction times
removes $75$--$98\%$ of it. Sizes and packetisation do not enter: decoupling transaction
sizes from timing, merging each transaction into one packet, or removing the spacing
inside a split transaction leaves the tail within $\pm 15\%$ of its value at every service
time, at the ZN slope as at the NQ slope. And the clustering of transactions --- the
ordering that TG destroys --- carries $20\%$ of the tail at $16\,\mu\mathrm{s}$, $40\%$ at
$32$, $63\%$ at $64$ and $78\%$ at $128\,\mu\mathrm{s}$, the same profile the packet-level
gap shuffle gave in Table~\ref{tab:nulls}. The remainder, dominant at HFT service times,
is the surplus of tight gaps between transactions over a Poisson stream at the same rate
(Table~\ref{tab:tx-gaps}), which on the publisher's clock sit at its spacing
(Table~\ref{tab:two-clocks}). That surplus says the transaction gap distribution is far
from exponential; it does not say what put the mass there, and a renewal stream with the
same gaps and no self-excitation (the gap shuffle) reproduces it and most of the tail at
$16\,\mu\mathrm{s}$. The attribution is therefore the following. The queueing tail behind
a single-threaded receiver on this feed is produced by the timing of matching-engine
transactions; it is not produced by packetisation, by the message count per packet, or by
the packet rate; it acts on a short service time through how often two transactions land
within one service time of each other, and on a long one through how long the runs of
them last, which is the self-excitation. Why two transactions land a few microseconds
apart is a question about the market, not about the receiver, and is left open
(Section~\ref{sec:concl-cannot}).

\subsection{Tandem, sharding and dispatch on the same $N$ cores}
\label{sec:results-equal-core}

The tandem of Table~\ref{tab:main-corpus} runs on $N$ cores and the single thread on
one, so part of what it buys is capacity: its throughput is $N/T$ against $1/T$, and more
capacity shortens queues under any arrival law. The design question is whether a chain
of stages is the right way to spend the same $N$ cores, and the two alternatives are the
ones HFT systems use. \emph{Sharding} runs $N$ independent single-thread loops,
each owning a disjoint slice of the stream, by instrument or by channel: full service $T$
per message and no hops, so it has the lower median, and its tail depends on whether
a burst lands on one shard or spreads over several. \emph{Dispatch}, an M/D/$N$ queue,
puts one queue in front of $N$ identical servers each taking a whole packet at service
$T$: the same capacity $N/T$ as the tandem and no hops, so a burst of $k$ packets drains
in about the same time, while each packet's own latency stays $T$ rather than
$T + (N-1)h$.

Sharding is not available on this corpus: a CME burst is one instrument's order flow, so
sharding by instrument sends the whole burst to one core at service $T$ and relieves
nothing, and the corpus is a single instrument. Dispatch is available for any stage that
holds no state from one packet to the next --- SBE decode is such a stage, order-book
application is not --- provided its output is put back in order. Two comparisons follow.
Table~\ref{tab:equal-core} puts dispatch beside the tandem with no ingress hop, no egress
hop and no resequencing charged, which is the lowest latency dispatch could reach.
Table~\ref{tab:dispatch-costed} charges all three.

Without its costs, dispatch's $p_{99}$ is at or below the tandem's in every cell at

$48.0$ against $52.3$ at $32$, $171$ against $180$ at $64$, $869$ against $886$ at
$128\,\mu\mathrm{s}$), and its median is $T$ against the tandem's $T + (N-1)h$.

\begin{table}[tbp]
\centering

slope; corpus medians over 3512 windows, in \si{\micro\second}, $h = 1.7\,\mu\mathrm{s}$;
last column the share of packets that finish before a predecessor and wait in the
resequencer (its $p_{99}$ wait is $0.00\,\mu\mathrm{s}$ in every cell). Constant service
gives the same picture. What the table shows: dispatch's median is $T + 2h$ at every
$N$, against the tandem's $T + (N-1)h$, so dispatch is one hop slower at $N = 2$ and
faster from $N = 3$; at $N = 2$ the two are within $1.2\,\mu\mathrm{s}$ at $p_{99}$ for
$T \le 32\,\mu\mathrm{s}$ and dispatch is lower above; at $N \ge 4$ dispatch is lower on
both quantiles in every cell. Resequencing costs almost nothing, because a packet that
waited in the shared queue behind a burst leaves in the order it arrived. What follows: for
a stage that carries no state, dispatching whole packets to three or more cores is at
least as good as cutting it into the same number of stages; at two cores the two are
level, with the tandem one hop cheaper on the median. For a stage that carries state, the
order book above all, dispatch is not available and the tandem is the arrangement that
